Well, I'm here because... I keep having these strange moments. It's hard to explain, but sometimes I'll be sitting in my own house, or at my desk at the office, and everything just feels completely unreal. It's like I'm watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. Which is a terrible feeling when you're trying to work through a stack of 1040s, let me tell you. Tax season is right around the corner, and my clients are already starting to call. Jim, this guy who owns a local hardware store, he called me three times yesterday about his inventory depreciation. Three times! I've been doing his taxes for fifteen years, and he always panics right about now. He bought a boat last year and thinks he can write it off as a business expense because he entertained a client on it once. I had to explain to him—again—how the IRS views entertainment expenses now. It's just exhausting, you know? But yeah, anyway, these moments where I feel like I'm floating outside my own body... they're really starting to bother me.

Well, let's see... it's been about two weeks now. Yeah, right around two weeks. It all started ever since that... that incident where a client threatened me right there in my office. Which, honestly, I still can't believe happened. I mean, I've been a tax preparer for nearly thirty years. You deal with upset people, sure. Nobody likes paying the IRS. But this guy... he was furious about an audit notice. And the thing is, I warned him! I told him last year, I said, "You can't claim your golden retriever as a security system just because he barks at the mailman." But did he listen? No. They never listen. And then they get the letter from the IRS, and suddenly it's my fault. He came in, slamming his hands on my desk, knocking over my coffee—ruined a perfectly good stack of folders, by the way. My secretary, Brenda, she was terrified. Brenda's been with me for five years, lovely woman, makes the best snickerdoodles around the holidays. But she almost quit that day. I had to give her a bonus just to keep her. But yeah, ever since that afternoon with him... that's when this weird, floating, unreal feeling started happening.

Physical symptoms? Um... no, not that I can think of. I just get that weird floating feeling. Though, speaking of physical things, my lower back has been absolutely killing me lately, but that's probably just because of this new office chair I bought. I got it from this discount supply place over on 4th Street—Big Bob's Office Bonanza. Which is funny, because Big Bob is actually a very short man. Barely five foot four. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Anxious? Yeah... yeah, I do. Actually, it's pretty much all the time now. Even when I'm just sitting at home in Roanoke, miles away from the office. I'll be in my living room, and I catch myself tracking every little sound. Like, I hear a car door shut down the street, and my head snaps up. Or I hear footsteps on the porch, and I'm instantly scanning the room, figuring out where the exits are, like I need to be ready for someone to come at me again. 

Which is exhausting, let me tell you. Especially since our porch is made of this old wooden decking that creaks if a squirrel so much as looks at it. We've been meaning to get it replaced for years. My wife, Susan, she wants to go with that composite stuff, you know? The kind that's supposed to look like real wood but never rots or splinters. Trex, I think it's called. But I looked at the prices, and good lord, you'd think they were selling gold-plated two-by-fours. I told her we could just power-wash the old wood and put a nice coat of sealant on it, but she says it's an eyesore. She's been spending way too much time watching those home renovation shows on HGTV. You know the ones, where they buy a rundown shack and somehow turn it into a mansion in three weeks? It gives people completely unrealistic expectations about construction timelines. Our plumber took two months just to fix a leaky pipe in the guest bathroom! But anyway, yeah... I'm just constantly on edge, waiting for something bad to happen, even when I'm supposed to be relaxing.

Before? No, never. It's really just been going on for about two weeks now, ever since that client threatened me in my office. I've been a tax preparer for over two decades and I've never had someone blow up like that over a standard deduction. I mean, the tax code is the tax code, right? I don't write the IRS guidelines. 

Speaking of the IRS, they changed the formatting on the 1040 again this year. It is a complete nightmare for our software. We use this program, TaxPro Elite, and every time there's a mid-season update, it crashes the entire office network. Last Tuesday, I spent three hours on hold with their tech support. Three hours! And the hold music is this awful, repetitive smooth jazz track. Just a synthesizer and a saxophone playing the same four notes on a loop. I swear they do it on purpose. It's a psychological tactic to make you hang up so they don't have to deal with you. I finally got a hold of a guy named Brent, who sounded like he was eating a sandwich the whole time he was talking to me. You could hear him chewing! It's just so unprofessional. 

But anyway, what was the question? Oh, right, the floating thing. No, I never felt anything like that before. Just the last two weeks since the incident.

Avoid thinking or talking about it? No, not really. I mean, I don't try to block it out or anything. If someone asks, I'll tell them. I was actually just on the phone with my brother, Dave, the other night, and we were talking for like an hour. Well, mostly he was talking.

They always do that trick where they focus on the monthly payment instead of the total cost of the vehicle. They extend the loan term out to 72 or 84 months so the monthly number looks smaller, but you end up paying thousands more in interest over the life of the loan. I tried to explain the amortization schedule to him, but he just glazed over. People never want to look at the math until tax season rolls around and they wonder where all their money went. 

But... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, avoiding talking about the client. No, no. I don't avoid it. I just don't really try to hide from it or anything.

No, no, I haven't. I mean, I've definitely had stressful things happen in my life, but I never felt that weird disconnected, floating thing before. Like, back in 2018, my water heater burst while I was at the office during the peak of tax season. Flooded my entire finished basement. Talk about stressful. The drywall was ruined, the carpet was soaking wet... we had to rip everything right down to the studs. 

And dealing with the insurance adjuster was an absolute nightmare. Guy's name was Greg, wore these awful yellow suspenders. He kept trying to claim it was a "negligent maintenance" issue and therefore wasn't fully covered under our specific policy tier. Can you believe that? I had to pull out the original policy documents—which, thank god I keep meticulous records. I mean, I'm a tax guy, I have a fireproof filing cabinet at home organized by year, cross-referenced by category. I actually had to highlight the specific clause on sudden water discharge for him. Insurance companies, I swear, they just want to collect your premiums every month and then fight you tooth and nail when you actually need a payout. It's a racket. 

It took four months to get that basement put back together. We ended up putting in that luxury vinyl plank flooring instead of carpet the second time around. It's much better for moisture, obviously, and it actually looks just like real hardwood. You wouldn't even know the difference. My wife picked out this really nice distressed hickory color that completely brightened up the room... 

But, uh, I'm sorry, what was the question? Something about the basement? Oh, right, the floating feeling. No, no. Even with all the stress of the flood and arguing with Greg, I was completely grounded. Never felt anything like this before.

It's like I'm watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. Which is a terrible feeling when you're trying to work through a stack of 1040s, let me tell you. Tax season is right around the corner, and my clients are already starting to call. Jim, this guy who owns a local hardware store, he called me three times yesterday about his inventory depreciation. Three times! I've been doing his taxes for fifteen years, and he always panics right about now. He bought a boat last year and thinks he can write it off as a business expense because he entertained a client on it once. I had to explain to him—again—how the IRS views entertainment expenses now. It's just exhausting, you know? But yeah, anyway, these moments where I feel like I'm floating outside my own body... they're really starting to bother me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these moments where everything feels unreal?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been about two weeks now. Yeah, right around two weeks. It all started ever since that... that incident where a client threatened me right there in my office. Which, honestly, I still can't believe happened. I mean, I've been a tax preparer for nearly thirty years. You deal with upset people, sure. Nobody likes paying the IRS. But this guy... he was furious about an audit notice. And the thing is, I warned him! I told him last year, I said, \"You can't claim your golden retriever as a security system just because he barks at the mailman.\" But did he listen? No. They never listen. And then they get the letter from the IRS, and suddenly it's my fault. He came in, slamming his hands on my desk, knocking over my coffee—ruined a perfectly good stack of folders, by the way. My secretary, Brenda, she was terrified. Brenda's been with me for five years, lovely woman, makes the best snickerdoodles around the holidays. But she almost quit that day. I had to give her a bonus just to keep her. But yeah, ever since that afternoon with him... that's when this weird, floating, unreal feeling started happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms when you're having these unreal moments?\n\nPatient: Physical symptoms? Um... no, not that I can think of. I just get that weird floating feeling. Though, speaking of physical things, my lower back has been absolutely killing me lately, but that's probably just because of this new office chair I bought. I got it from this discount supply place over on 4th Street—Big Bob's Office Bonanza. Which is funny, because Big Bob is actually a very short man. Barely five foot four. Anyway, he promised me this chair had top-of-the-line lumbar support, but I swear it feels like a piece of plywood wrapped in cheap pleather. I really should have just ordered from the corporate catalog like Brenda told me to. She loves those catalogs. Every November, she goes through them with a pink highlighter, picking out sticky notes and those little rubber finger pads we use for sorting receipts. You know the ones? They look like tiny, bumpy thimbles. We must go through a hundred of those a season, what with all the paper cuts you get from W-2s. But, uh... what were you asking? Oh, right, my body during those moments. No, no racing heart or sweating or anything like that. Just the... the floating.\n\nDoctor: Do these unreal moments seem to happen at specific times or when you're doing certain things?\n\nPatient: Well, let's think... specific times. You know, I haven't really noticed a pattern. It just sort of happens whenever. Like yesterday, I was just looking out the window at the parking lot. Which, by the way, they finally repaved last weekend. It's about time, too. The potholes out there were getting ridiculous. I lost a hubcap in one back in November. A genuine Ford hubcap, not one of those cheap knockoffs you buy at the auto parts store. I tried to get the property manager, Gary, to reimburse me for it, but he just gave me the runaround. Gary's always doing that. He's one of those guys who talks a lot but never actually says anything, you know? He wears these incredibly loud ties—polka dots, neon stripes, you name it. I think he thinks it makes him look approachable, but really it just gives you a headache if you stare at him too long. Anyway, I was looking at the new asphalt, and suddenly I just felt like I was floating up near the ceiling tiles. So, no, I can't say it's tied to any specific activity. It just... sneaks up on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about the incident with the client who threatened you?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Yeah... yeah, I do. Actually, it's pretty much all the time now. Even when I'm just sitting at home in Roanoke, miles away from the office. I'll be in my living room, and I catch myself tracking every little sound. Like, I hear a car door shut down the street, and my head snaps up. Or I hear footsteps on the porch, and I'm instantly scanning the room, figuring out where the exits are, like I need to be ready for someone to come at me again. \n\nWhich is exhausting, let me tell you. Especially since our porch is made of this old wooden decking that creaks if a squirrel so much as looks at it. We've been meaning to get it replaced for years. My wife, Susan, she wants to go with that composite stuff, you know? The kind that's supposed to look like real wood but never rots or splinters. Trex, I think it's called. But I looked at the prices, and good lord, you'd think they were selling gold-plated two-by-fours. I told her we could just power-wash the old wood and put a nice coat of sealant on it, but she says it's an eyesore. She's been spending way too much time watching those home renovation shows on HGTV. You know the ones, where they buy a rundown shack and somehow turn it into a mansion in three weeks? It gives people completely unrealistic expectations about construction timelines. Our plumber took two months just to fix a leaky pipe in the guest bathroom! But anyway, yeah... I'm just constantly on edge, waiting for something bad to happen, even when I'm supposed to be relaxing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, people, or activities since the incident with the client who threatened you?\n\nPatient: Avoiding things? Um... no, not really. I mean, I still go to work, I still go to the grocery store. Though, speaking of the grocery store, I was at Kroger the other day, the one over on Elm Avenue. They completely rearranged the aisles. It's infuriating! I spent twenty minutes just trying to find the canned tomatoes. Why do they do that? You finally learn where everything is, and then some corporate manager decides the pasta needs to be next to the baking supplies instead of the sauces. It makes no sense. I was talking to the checkout girl about it—she's a sweet kid, maybe nineteen, always wears these oversized glasses. She said they do it so you have to walk past more items and hopefully buy things you didn't come in for. Which, I suppose, is a smart business strategy, but it's incredibly frustrating when you're just trying to make a quick chili. I make a really good chili, by the way. Secret is a dash of cinnamon. My mother taught me that. She used to make huge pots of it every winter... But, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, avoiding things. No, I haven't really been avoiding anything. I just go about my day, you know?\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have these unreal or floating feelings before the client threatened you in your office?\n\nPatient: Before? No, never. It's really just been going on for about two weeks now, ever since that client threatened me in my office. I've been a tax preparer for over two decades and I've never had someone blow up like that over a standard deduction. I mean, the tax code is the tax code, right? I don't write the IRS guidelines. \n\nSpeaking of the IRS, they changed the formatting on the 1040 again this year. It is a complete nightmare for our software. We use this program, TaxPro Elite, and every time there's a mid-season update, it crashes the entire office network. Last Tuesday, I spent three hours on hold with their tech support. Three hours! And the hold music is this awful, repetitive smooth jazz track. Just a synthesizer and a saxophone playing the same four notes on a loop. I swear they do it on purpose. It's a psychological tactic to make you hang up so they don't have to deal with you. I finally got a hold of a guy named Brent, who sounded like he was eating a sandwich the whole time he was talking to me. You could hear him chewing! It's just so unprofessional. \n\nBut anyway, what was the question? Oh, right, the floating thing. No, I never felt anything like that before. Just the last two weeks since the incident.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to avoid thinking or talking about what happened with that client who threatened you?\n\nPatient: Avoid thinking or talking about it? No, not really. I mean, I don't try to block it out or anything. If someone asks, I'll tell them. I was actually just on the phone with my brother, Dave, the other night, and we were talking for like an hour. Well, mostly he was talking. \n\nHe just bought a new truck. A Ford F-150. And let me tell you, the interest rates right now are absolutely criminal. As a tax guy, I look at the financing terms he agreed to, and it just makes my stomach turn. I told him, 'Dave, you're paying almost eight percent on a depreciating asset!' But he wouldn't listen. He just had to have the heated seats and the towing package. He doesn't even own a boat! What is he towing? The dealership saw him coming a mile away. They always do that trick where they focus on the monthly payment instead of the total cost of the vehicle. They extend the loan term out to 72 or 84 months so the monthly number looks smaller, but you end up paying thousands more in interest over the life of the loan. I tried to explain the amortization schedule to him, but he just glazed over. People never want to look at the math until tax season rolls around and they wonder where all their money went. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, avoiding talking about the client. No, no. I don't avoid it. I just don't really try to hide from it or anything.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience anything similar to these unreal or floating feelings during any other stressful or traumatic event in your life before this incident with the client?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't. I mean, I've definitely had stressful things happen in my life, but I never felt that weird disconnected, floating thing before. Like, back in 2018, my water heater burst while I was at the office during the peak of tax season. Flooded my entire finished basement. Talk about stressful. The drywall was ruined, the carpet was soaking wet... we had to rip everything right down to the studs. \n\nAnd dealing with the insurance adjuster was an absolute nightmare. Guy's name was Greg, wore these awful yellow suspenders. He kept trying to claim it was a \"negligent maintenance\" issue and therefore wasn't fully covered under our specific policy tier. Can you believe that? I had to pull out the original policy documents—which, thank god I keep meticulous records. I mean, I'm a tax guy, I have a fireproof filing cabinet at home organized by year, cross-referenced by category. I actually had to highlight the specific clause on sudden water discharge for him. Insurance companies, I swear, they just want to collect your premiums every month and then fight you tooth and nail when you actually need a payout. It's a racket. \n\nIt took four months to get that basement put back together. We ended up putting in that luxury vinyl plank flooring instead of carpet the second time around. It's much better for moisture, obviously, and it actually looks just like real hardwood. You wouldn't even know the difference. My wife picked out this really nice distressed hickory color that completely brightened up the room... \n\nBut, uh, I'm sorry, what was the question? Something about the basement? Oh, right, the floating feeling. No, no. Even with all the stress of the flood and arguing with Greg, I was completely grounded. Never felt anything like this before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I told him, 'Dave, you're paying almost eight percent on a depreciating asset!' But he wouldn't listen. He just had to have the heated seats and the towing package. He doesn't even own a boat! What is he towing? The dealership saw him coming a mile away. They always do that trick where they focus on the monthly payment instead of the total cost of the vehicle. They extend the loan term out to 72 or 84 months so the monthly number looks smaller, but you end up paying thousands more in interest over the life of the loan. I tried to explain the amortization schedule to him, but he just glazed over. People never want to look at the math until tax season rolls around and they wonder where all their money went. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, avoiding talking about the client. No, no. I don't avoid it. I just don't really try to hide from it or anything.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience anything similar to these unreal or floating feelings during any other stressful or traumatic event in your life before this incident with the client?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't. I mean, I've definitely had stressful things happen in my life, but I never felt that weird disconnected, floating thing before. Like, back in 2018, my water heater burst while I was at the office during the peak of tax season. Flooded my entire finished basement. Talk about stressful. The drywall was ruined, the carpet was soaking wet... we had to rip everything right down to the studs. \n\nAnd dealing with the insurance adjuster was an absolute nightmare. Guy's name was Greg, wore these awful yellow suspenders. He kept trying to claim it was a \"negligent maintenance\" issue and therefore wasn't fully covered under our specific policy tier. Can you believe that? I had to pull out the original policy documents—which, thank god I keep meticulous records. I mean, I'm a tax guy, I have a fireproof filing cabinet at home organized by year, cross-referenced by category. I actually had to highlight the specific clause on sudden water discharge for him. Insurance companies, I swear, they just want to collect your premiums every month and then fight you tooth and nail when you actually need a payout. It's a racket. \n\nIt took four months to get that basement put back together. We ended up putting in that luxury vinyl plank flooring instead of carpet the second time around. It's much better for moisture, obviously, and it actually looks just like real hardwood. You wouldn't even know the difference. My wife picked out this really nice distressed hickory color that completely brightened up the room... \n\nBut, uh, I'm sorry, what was the question? Something about the basement? Oh, right, the floating feeling. No, no. Even with all the stress of the flood and arguing with Greg, I was completely grounded. Never felt anything like this before.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find that these unreal or floating feelings happen more often when you're reminded of the client who threatened you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Well, I guess... yeah, maybe a little bit. It's weird. Like, what brings me in is that I keep having these moments where my own house or office feels unreal. Like I'm watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. It definitely happens when I think about the guy, but sometimes it just happens randomly, too. 

Like the other day, I was sitting in my living room, looking at the coffee table. We just got a new coffee table, actually. Solid oak. My wife found it at this antique store out in the country, about an hour drive from here. The guy who runs the place, his name is Arthur, he's got this incredible collection of mid-century modern stuff. But the prices! You'd think he was selling gold bricks instead of furniture. We haggled with him for a good forty-five minutes. I told him, 'Arthur, there's a scratch on the left leg, I'm not paying full price.' He tried to tell me it was 'patina' and added to the character of the piece. Patina! It's a scratch from someone dragging a box across it. I know the difference between natural aging and careless handling. 

Anyway, we finally got it home, and I was just sitting there looking at it, and suddenly everything felt... fake. Like I wasn't really in the room. I was just hovering somewhere near the ceiling, watching myself look at the coffee table. It's the strangest sensation. 

Wait, did you ask about the client? Yeah, so, I mean, it happens then, too. But the coffee table thing was definitely real. Or... unreal, I guess.

But yeah, ever since that afternoon with him... that's when this weird, floating, unreal feeling started happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms when you're having these unreal moments?\n\nPatient: Physical symptoms? Um... no, not that I can think of. I just get that weird floating feeling. Though, speaking of physical things, my lower back has been absolutely killing me lately, but that's probably just because of this new office chair I bought. I got it from this discount supply place over on 4th Street—Big Bob's Office Bonanza. Which is funny, because Big Bob is actually a very short man. Barely five foot four. Anyway, he promised me this chair had top-of-the-line lumbar support, but I swear it feels like a piece of plywood wrapped in cheap pleather. I really should have just ordered from the corporate catalog like Brenda told me to. She loves those catalogs. Every November, she goes through them with a pink highlighter, picking out sticky notes and those little rubber finger pads we use for sorting receipts. You know the ones? They look like tiny, bumpy thimbles. We must go through a hundred of those a season, what with all the paper cuts you get from W-2s. But, uh... what were you asking? Oh, right, my body during those moments. No, no racing heart or sweating or anything like that. Just the... the floating.\n\nDoctor: Do these unreal moments seem to happen at specific times or when you're doing certain things?\n\nPatient: Well, let's think... specific times. You know, I haven't really noticed a pattern. It just sort of happens whenever. Like yesterday, I was just looking out the window at the parking lot. Which, by the way, they finally repaved last weekend. It's about time, too. The potholes out there were getting ridiculous. I lost a hubcap in one back in November. A genuine Ford hubcap, not one of those cheap knockoffs you buy at the auto parts store. I tried to get the property manager, Gary, to reimburse me for it, but he just gave me the runaround. Gary's always doing that. He's one of those guys who talks a lot but never actually says anything, you know? He wears these incredibly loud ties—polka dots, neon stripes, you name it. I think he thinks it makes him look approachable, but really it just gives you a headache if you stare at him too long. Anyway, I was looking at the new asphalt, and suddenly I just felt like I was floating up near the ceiling tiles. So, no, I can't say it's tied to any specific activity. It just... sneaks up on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about the incident with the client who threatened you?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Yeah... yeah, I do. Actually, it's pretty much all the time now. Even when I'm just sitting at home in Roanoke, miles away from the office. I'll be in my living room, and I catch myself tracking every little sound. Like, I hear a car door shut down the street, and my head snaps up. Or I hear footsteps on the porch, and I'm instantly scanning the room, figuring out where the exits are, like I need to be ready for someone to come at me again. \n\nWhich is exhausting, let me tell you. Especially since our porch is made of this old wooden decking that creaks if a squirrel so much as looks at it. We've been meaning to get it replaced for years. My wife, Susan, she wants to go with that composite stuff, you know? The kind that's supposed to look like real wood but never rots or splinters. Trex, I think it's called. But I looked at the prices, and good lord, you'd think they were selling gold-plated two-by-fours. I told her we could just power-wash the old wood and put a nice coat of sealant on it, but she says it's an eyesore. She's been spending way too much time watching those home renovation shows on HGTV. You know the ones, where they buy a rundown shack and somehow turn it into a mansion in three weeks? It gives people completely unrealistic expectations about construction timelines. Our plumber took two months just to fix a leaky pipe in the guest bathroom! But anyway, yeah... I'm just constantly on edge, waiting for something bad to happen, even when I'm supposed to be relaxing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, people, or activities since the incident with the client who threatened you?\n\nPatient: Avoiding things? Um... no, not really. I mean, I still go to work, I still go to the grocery store. Though, speaking of the grocery store, I was at Kroger the other day, the one over on Elm Avenue. They completely rearranged the aisles. It's infuriating! I spent twenty minutes just trying to find the canned tomatoes. Why do they do that? You finally learn where everything is, and then some corporate manager decides the pasta needs to be next to the baking supplies instead of the sauces. It makes no sense. I was talking to the checkout girl about it—she's a sweet kid, maybe nineteen, always wears these oversized glasses. She said they do it so you have to walk past more items and hopefully buy things you didn't come in for. Which, I suppose, is a smart business strategy, but it's incredibly frustrating when you're just trying to make a quick chili. I make a really good chili, by the way. Secret is a dash of cinnamon. My mother taught me that. She used to make huge pots of it every winter... But, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, avoiding things. No, I haven't really been avoiding anything. I just go about my day, you know?\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have these unreal or floating feelings before the client threatened you in your office?\n\nPatient: Before? No, never. It's really just been going on for about two weeks now, ever since that client threatened me in my office. I've been a tax preparer for over two decades and I've never had someone blow up like that over a standard deduction. I mean, the tax code is the tax code, right? I don't write the IRS guidelines. \n\nSpeaking of the IRS, they changed the formatting on the 1040 again this year. It is a complete nightmare for our software. We use this program, TaxPro Elite, and every time there's a mid-season update, it crashes the entire office network. Last Tuesday, I spent three hours on hold with their tech support. Three hours! And the hold music is this awful, repetitive smooth jazz track. Just a synthesizer and a saxophone playing the same four notes on a loop. I swear they do it on purpose. It's a psychological tactic to make you hang up so they don't have to deal with you. I finally got a hold of a guy named Brent, who sounded like he was eating a sandwich the whole time he was talking to me. You could hear him chewing! It's just so unprofessional. \n\nBut anyway, what was the question? Oh, right, the floating thing. No, I never felt anything like that before. Just the last two weeks since the incident.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to avoid thinking or talking about what happened with that client who threatened you?\n\nPatient: Avoid thinking or talking about it? No, not really. I mean, I don't try to block it out or anything. If someone asks, I'll tell them. I was actually just on the phone with my brother, Dave, the other night, and we were talking for like an hour. Well, mostly he was talking. \n\nHe just bought a new truck. A Ford F-150. And let me tell you, the interest rates right now are absolutely criminal. As a tax guy, I look at the financing terms he agreed to, and it just makes my stomach turn. I told him, 'Dave, you're paying almost eight percent on a depreciating asset!' But he wouldn't listen. He just had to have the heated seats and the towing package. He doesn't even own a boat! What is he towing? The dealership saw him coming a mile away. They always do that trick where they focus on the monthly payment instead of the total cost of the vehicle. They extend the loan term out to 72 or 84 months so the monthly number looks smaller, but you end up paying thousands more in interest over the life of the loan. I tried to explain the amortization schedule to him, but he just glazed over. People never want to look at the math until tax season rolls around and they wonder where all their money went. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, avoiding talking about the client. No, no. I don't avoid it. I just don't really try to hide from it or anything.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience anything similar to these unreal or floating feelings during any other stressful or traumatic event in your life before this incident with the client?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't. I mean, I've definitely had stressful things happen in my life, but I never felt that weird disconnected, floating thing before. Like, back in 2018, my water heater burst while I was at the office during the peak of tax season. Flooded my entire finished basement. Talk about stressful. The drywall was ruined, the carpet was soaking wet... we had to rip everything right down to the studs. \n\nAnd dealing with the insurance adjuster was an absolute nightmare. Guy's name was Greg, wore these awful yellow suspenders. He kept trying to claim it was a \"negligent maintenance\" issue and therefore wasn't fully covered under our specific policy tier. Can you believe that? I had to pull out the original policy documents—which, thank god I keep meticulous records. I mean, I'm a tax guy, I have a fireproof filing cabinet at home organized by year, cross-referenced by category. I actually had to highlight the specific clause on sudden water discharge for him. Insurance companies, I swear, they just want to collect your premiums every month and then fight you tooth and nail when you actually need a payout. It's a racket. \n\nIt took four months to get that basement put back together. We ended up putting in that luxury vinyl plank flooring instead of carpet the second time around. It's much better for moisture, obviously, and it actually looks just like real hardwood. You wouldn't even know the difference. My wife picked out this really nice distressed hickory color that completely brightened up the room... \n\nBut, uh, I'm sorry, what was the question? Something about the basement? Oh, right, the floating feeling. No, no. Even with all the stress of the flood and arguing with Greg, I was completely grounded. Never felt anything like this before.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these unreal or floating feelings happen more often when you're reminded of the client who threatened you?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... yeah, maybe a little bit. It's weird. Like, what brings me in is that I keep having these moments where my own house or office feels unreal. Like I'm watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. It definitely happens when I think about the guy, but sometimes it just happens randomly, too. \n\nLike the other day, I was sitting in my living room, looking at the coffee table. We just got a new coffee table, actually. Solid oak. My wife found it at this antique store out in the country, about an hour drive from here. The guy who runs the place, his name is Arthur, he's got this incredible collection of mid-century modern stuff. But the prices! You'd think he was selling gold bricks instead of furniture. We haggled with him for a good forty-five minutes. I told him, 'Arthur, there's a scratch on the left leg, I'm not paying full price.' He tried to tell me it was 'patina' and added to the character of the piece. Patina! It's a scratch from someone dragging a box across it. I know the difference between natural aging and careless handling. \n\nAnyway, we finally got it home, and I was just sitting there looking at it, and suddenly everything felt... fake. Like I wasn't really in the room. I was just hovering somewhere near the ceiling, watching myself look at the coffee table. It's the strangest sensation. \n\nWait, did you ask about the client? Yeah, so, I mean, it happens then, too. But the coffee table thing was definitely real. Or... unreal, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. The office is the worst for it. Like I was saying before, what brings me in is that I keep having these moments where my own house or office feels unreal. Like I'm watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. It happened just Tuesday when I was sitting at my desk. 

I was trying to review a client's Schedule C. The guy runs a small landscaping business, and his bookkeeping is a total disaster. He brings me his receipts in a literal shoebox. A Nike box! I've told him a hundred times, "Frank, you need to use QuickBooks, or at least a spreadsheet." But no, every April, it's the shoebox. And half the receipts are faded because he leaves them on the dashboard of his truck in the sun. Thermal paper doesn't survive that! I spend hours just trying to decipher if a receipt is from Home Depot or a gas station. And then he wants to deduct his dog's food as a security expense because the dog rides in the truck with him. I had to explain to him the IRS's definition of an "ordinary and necessary" business expense. They don't mess around with that stuff. If you get audited and you're trying to write off Purina Dog Chow for a golden retriever, they're going to throw the book at you. 

Anyway, I was staring at this faded receipt, and suddenly... I just felt like I wasn't at my desk anymore. I was looking at myself looking at the receipt. The whole office just felt like a movie set or something. So, yeah, it definitely happens a lot in the office.

Or I hear footsteps on the porch, and I'm instantly scanning the room, figuring out where the exits are, like I need to be ready for someone to come at me again. \n\nWhich is exhausting, let me tell you. Especially since our porch is made of this old wooden decking that creaks if a squirrel so much as looks at it. We've been meaning to get it replaced for years. My wife, Susan, she wants to go with that composite stuff, you know? The kind that's supposed to look like real wood but never rots or splinters. Trex, I think it's called. But I looked at the prices, and good lord, you'd think they were selling gold-plated two-by-fours. I told her we could just power-wash the old wood and put a nice coat of sealant on it, but she says it's an eyesore. She's been spending way too much time watching those home renovation shows on HGTV. You know the ones, where they buy a rundown shack and somehow turn it into a mansion in three weeks? It gives people completely unrealistic expectations about construction timelines. Our plumber took two months just to fix a leaky pipe in the guest bathroom! But anyway, yeah... I'm just constantly on edge, waiting for something bad to happen, even when I'm supposed to be relaxing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, people, or activities since the incident with the client who threatened you?\n\nPatient: Avoiding things? Um... no, not really. I mean, I still go to work, I still go to the grocery store. Though, speaking of the grocery store, I was at Kroger the other day, the one over on Elm Avenue. They completely rearranged the aisles. It's infuriating! I spent twenty minutes just trying to find the canned tomatoes. Why do they do that? You finally learn where everything is, and then some corporate manager decides the pasta needs to be next to the baking supplies instead of the sauces. It makes no sense. I was talking to the checkout girl about it—she's a sweet kid, maybe nineteen, always wears these oversized glasses. She said they do it so you have to walk past more items and hopefully buy things you didn't come in for. Which, I suppose, is a smart business strategy, but it's incredibly frustrating when you're just trying to make a quick chili. I make a really good chili, by the way. Secret is a dash of cinnamon. My mother taught me that. She used to make huge pots of it every winter... But, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, avoiding things. No, I haven't really been avoiding anything. I just go about my day, you know?\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have these unreal or floating feelings before the client threatened you in your office?\n\nPatient: Before? No, never. It's really just been going on for about two weeks now, ever since that client threatened me in my office. I've been a tax preparer for over two decades and I've never had someone blow up like that over a standard deduction. I mean, the tax code is the tax code, right? I don't write the IRS guidelines. \n\nSpeaking of the IRS, they changed the formatting on the 1040 again this year. It is a complete nightmare for our software. We use this program, TaxPro Elite, and every time there's a mid-season update, it crashes the entire office network. Last Tuesday, I spent three hours on hold with their tech support. Three hours! And the hold music is this awful, repetitive smooth jazz track. Just a synthesizer and a saxophone playing the same four notes on a loop. I swear they do it on purpose. It's a psychological tactic to make you hang up so they don't have to deal with you. It's just so unprofessional. \n\nBut anyway, what was the question? Oh, right, the floating thing. No, I never felt anything like that before. Just the last two weeks since the incident.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to avoid thinking or talking about what happened with that client who threatened you?\n\nPatient: Avoid thinking or talking about it? No, not really. I mean, I don't try to block it out or anything. If someone asks, I'll tell them. I was actually just on the phone with my brother, Dave, the other night, and we were talking for like an hour. Well, mostly he was talking. \n\nHe just bought a new truck. A Ford F-150. And let me tell you, the interest rates right now are absolutely criminal. As a tax guy, I look at the financing terms he agreed to, and it just makes my stomach turn. I told him, 'Dave, you're paying almost eight percent on a depreciating asset!' But he wouldn't listen. He just had to have the heated seats and the towing package. He doesn't even own a boat! What is he towing? The dealership saw him coming a mile away. They always do that trick where they focus on the monthly payment instead of the total cost of the vehicle. They extend the loan term out to 72 or 84 months so the monthly number looks smaller, but you end up paying thousands more in interest over the life of the loan. I tried to explain the amortization schedule to him, but he just glazed over. People never want to look at the math until tax season rolls around and they wonder where all their money went. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, avoiding talking about the client. No, no. I don't avoid it. I just don't really try to hide from it or anything.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience anything similar to these unreal or floating feelings during any other stressful or traumatic event in your life before this incident with the client?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't. I mean, I've definitely had stressful things happen in my life, but I never felt that weird disconnected, floating thing before. Like, back in 2018, my water heater burst while I was at the office during the peak of tax season. Flooded my entire finished basement. Talk about stressful. The drywall was ruined, the carpet was soaking wet... we had to rip everything right down to the studs. \n\nAnd dealing with the insurance adjuster was an absolute nightmare. Guy's name was Greg, wore these awful yellow suspenders. He kept trying to claim it was a \"negligent maintenance\" issue and therefore wasn't fully covered under our specific policy tier. Can you believe that? I had to pull out the original policy documents—which, thank god I keep meticulous records. I mean, I'm a tax guy, I have a fireproof filing cabinet at home organized by year, cross-referenced by category. I actually had to highlight the specific clause on sudden water discharge for him. Insurance companies, I swear, they just want to collect your premiums every month and then fight you tooth and nail when you actually need a payout. It's a racket. \n\nIt took four months to get that basement put back together. We ended up putting in that luxury vinyl plank flooring instead of carpet the second time around. It's much better for moisture, obviously, and it actually looks just like real hardwood. You wouldn't even know the difference. My wife picked out this really nice distressed hickory color that completely brightened up the room... \n\nBut, uh, I'm sorry, what was the question? Something about the basement? Oh, right, the floating feeling. No, no. Even with all the stress of the flood and arguing with Greg, I was completely grounded. Never felt anything like this before.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these unreal or floating feelings happen more often when you're reminded of the client who threatened you?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... yeah, maybe a little bit. It's weird. Like, what brings me in is that I keep having these moments where my own house or office feels unreal. Like I'm watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. It definitely happens when I think about the guy, but sometimes it just happens randomly, too. \n\nLike the other day, I was sitting in my living room, looking at the coffee table. We just got a new coffee table, actually. Solid oak. My wife found it at this antique store out in the country, about an hour drive from here. The guy who runs the place, his name is Arthur, he's got this incredible collection of mid-century modern stuff. But the prices! You'd think he was selling gold bricks instead of furniture. We haggled with him for a good forty-five minutes. I told him, 'Arthur, there's a scratch on the left leg, I'm not paying full price.' He tried to tell me it was 'patina' and added to the character of the piece. Patina! It's a scratch from someone dragging a box across it. I know the difference between natural aging and careless handling. \n\nAnyway, we finally got it home, and I was just sitting there looking at it, and suddenly everything felt... fake. Like I wasn't really in the room. I was just hovering somewhere near the ceiling, watching myself look at the coffee table. It's the strangest sensation. \n\nWait, did you ask about the client? Yeah, so, I mean, it happens then, too. But the coffee table thing was definitely real. Or... unreal, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the unreal feelings happen more often when you're in your office where the client threatened you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like I'm watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. It happened just Tuesday when I was sitting at my desk. \n\nI was trying to review a client's Schedule C. Gary's always doing that. He's one of those guys who talks a lot but never actually says anything, you know? He wears these incredibly loud ties—polka dots, neon stripes, you name it. Especially since our porch is made of this old wooden decking that creaks if a squirrel so much as looks at it. We've been meaning to get it replaced for years. My wife, Susan, she wants to go with that composite stuff, you know? The kind that's supposed to look like real wood but never rots or splinters. Trex, I think it's called. But I looked at the prices, and good lord, you'd think they were selling gold-plated two-by-fours. I told her we could just power-wash the old wood and put a nice coat of sealant on it, but she says it's an eyesore. She's been spending way too much time watching those home renovation shows on HGTV. You know the ones, where they buy a rundown shack and somehow turn it into a mansion in three weeks? It gives people completely unrealistic expectations about construction timelines. Our plumber took two months just to fix a leaky pipe in the guest bathroom! But anyway, yeah... I'm just constantly on edge, waiting for something bad to happen, even when I'm supposed to be relaxing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, people, or activities since the incident with the client who threatened you?\n\nPatient: Avoiding things? Um... no, not really. I mean, I still go to work, I still go to the grocery store. Though, speaking of the grocery store, I was at Kroger the other day, the one over on Elm Avenue. They completely rearranged the aisles. It's infuriating! I spent twenty minutes just trying to find the canned tomatoes. Why do they do that? You finally learn where everything is, and then some corporate manager decides the pasta needs to be next to the baking supplies instead of the sauces. It makes no sense. I was talking to the checkout girl about it—she's a sweet kid, maybe nineteen, always wears these oversized glasses. She said they do it so you have to walk past more items and hopefully buy things you didn't come in for. Which, I suppose, is a smart business strategy, but it's incredibly frustrating when you're just trying to make a quick chili. I make a really good chili, by the way. Secret is a dash of cinnamon. I just go about my day, you know?\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have these unreal or floating feelings before the client threatened you in your office?\n\nPatient: Before? No, never. It's really just been going on for about two weeks now, ever since that client threatened me in my office. I've been a tax preparer for over two decades and I've never had someone blow up like that over a standard deduction. I mean, the tax code is the tax code, right? I don't write the IRS guidelines. \n\nSpeaking of the IRS, they changed the formatting on the 1040 again this year. It is a complete nightmare for our software. We use this program, TaxPro Elite, and every time there's a mid-season update, it crashes the entire office network. Last Tuesday, I spent three hours on hold with their tech support. Three hours! And the hold music is this awful, repetitive smooth jazz track. Just a synthesizer and a saxophone playing the same four notes on a loop. I swear they do it on purpose. It's a psychological tactic to make you hang up so they don't have to deal with you. I finally got a hold of a guy named Brent, who sounded like he was eating a sandwich the whole time he was talking to me. You could hear him chewing! Just the last two weeks since the incident.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to avoid thinking or talking about what happened with that client who threatened you?\n\nPatient: Avoid thinking or talking about it? No, not really. I mean, I don't try to block it out or anything. If someone asks, I'll tell them. I was actually just on the phone with my brother, Dave, the other night, and we were talking for like an hour. Well, mostly he was talking. \n\nHe just bought a new truck. A Ford F-150. And let me tell you, the interest rates right now are absolutely criminal. As a tax guy, I look at the financing terms he agreed to, and it just makes my stomach turn. I told him, 'Dave, you're paying almost eight percent on a depreciating asset!' But he wouldn't listen. He just had to have the heated seats and the towing package. He doesn't even own a boat! What is he towing? The dealership saw him coming a mile away. They always do that trick where they focus on the monthly payment instead of the total cost of the vehicle. They extend the loan term out to 72 or 84 months so the monthly number looks smaller, but you end up paying thousands more in interest over the life of the loan. I tried to explain the amortization schedule to him, but he just glazed over. People never want to look at the math until tax season rolls around and they wonder where all their money went. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, avoiding talking about the client. No, no. I don't avoid it. I just don't really try to hide from it or anything.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience anything similar to these unreal or floating feelings during any other stressful or traumatic event in your life before this incident with the client?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't. I mean, I've definitely had stressful things happen in my life, but I never felt that weird disconnected, floating thing before. Like, back in 2018, my water heater burst while I was at the office during the peak of tax season. Flooded my entire finished basement. Talk about stressful. The drywall was ruined, the carpet was soaking wet... we had to rip everything right down to the studs. \n\nAnd dealing with the insurance adjuster was an absolute nightmare. Guy's name was Greg, wore these awful yellow suspenders. He kept trying to claim it was a \"negligent maintenance\" issue and therefore wasn't fully covered under our specific policy tier. Can you believe that? I had to pull out the original policy documents—which, thank god I keep meticulous records. I mean, I'm a tax guy, I have a fireproof filing cabinet at home organized by year, cross-referenced by category. I actually had to highlight the specific clause on sudden water discharge for him. Insurance companies, I swear, they just want to collect your premiums every month and then fight you tooth and nail when you actually need a payout. It's a racket. \n\nIt took four months to get that basement put back together. We ended up putting in that luxury vinyl plank flooring instead of carpet the second time around. It's much better for moisture, obviously, and it actually looks just like real hardwood. You wouldn't even know the difference. My wife picked out this really nice distressed hickory color that completely brightened up the room... \n\nBut, uh, I'm sorry, what was the question? Something about the basement? Oh, right, the floating feeling. No, no. Even with all the stress of the flood and arguing with Greg, I was completely grounded. Never felt anything like this before.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these unreal or floating feelings happen more often when you're reminded of the client who threatened you?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... yeah, maybe a little bit. It's weird. Like, what brings me in is that I keep having these moments where my own house or office feels unreal. Like I'm watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. It definitely happens when I think about the guy, but sometimes it just happens randomly, too. \n\nLike the other day, I was sitting in my living room, looking at the coffee table. We just got a new coffee table, actually. Solid oak. My wife found it at this antique store out in the country, about an hour drive from here. The guy who runs the place, his name is Arthur, he's got this incredible collection of mid-century modern stuff. But the prices! You'd think he was selling gold bricks instead of furniture. We haggled with him for a good forty-five minutes. I told him, 'Arthur, there's a scratch on the left leg, I'm not paying full price.' He tried to tell me it was 'patina' and added to the character of the piece. Patina! It's a scratch from someone dragging a box across it. I know the difference between natural aging and careless handling. \n\nAnyway, we finally got it home, and I was just sitting there looking at it, and suddenly everything felt... fake. Like I wasn't really in the room. I was just hovering somewhere near the ceiling, watching myself look at the coffee table. It's the strangest sensation. \n\nWait, did you ask about the client? Yeah, so, I mean, it happens then, too. But the coffee table thing was definitely real. Or... unreal, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the unreal feelings happen more often when you're in your office where the client threatened you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. The office is the worst for it. Like I was saying before, what brings me in is that I keep having these moments where my own house or office feels unreal. Like I'm watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. It happened just Tuesday when I was sitting at my desk. \n\nI was trying to review a client's Schedule C. The guy runs a small landscaping business, and his bookkeeping is a total disaster. He brings me his receipts in a literal shoebox. A Nike box! I've told him a hundred times, \"Frank, you need to use QuickBooks, or at least a spreadsheet.\" But no, every April, it's the shoebox. And half the receipts are faded because he leaves them on the dashboard of his truck in the sun. Thermal paper doesn't survive that! I spend hours just trying to decipher if a receipt is from Home Depot or a gas station. And then he wants to deduct his dog's food as a security expense because the dog rides in the truck with him. I had to explain to him the IRS's definition of an \"ordinary and necessary\" business expense. They don't mess around with that stuff. If you get audited and you're trying to write off Purina Dog Chow for a golden retriever, they're going to throw the book at you. \n\nAnyway, I was staring at this faded receipt, and suddenly... I just felt like I wasn't at my desk anymore. I was looking at myself looking at the receipt. The whole office just felt like a movie set or something. So, yeah, it definitely happens a lot in the office.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the unreal feelings happen more often when you see or think about things that remind you of the client who threatened you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like, even just seeing a certain type of folder sets it off. The guy had this thick, overstuffed manila envelope when he came in—you know the ones with the little red string you wrap around the cardboard circles? I usually buy those in bulk from Staples. Well, actually, I switched to Uline last year because Staples completely jacked up their free shipping minimums for business accounts. Uline is pretty good, though they send you this massive catalog every single month. It's literally the size of a phone book. \n\nDo they even make phone books anymore? I remember when they used to just chuck them onto your driveway in those clear plastic bags. My dad used to use two of the thick yellow pages just to prop up his computer monitor. He had one of those old beige CRT monitors that took up half the desk and weighed like forty pounds. You had to degauss it every once in a while, remember that? It made that loud *thunk* sound...\n\nWait, I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. Reminders of the client, right. Yeah, if I see one of those string envelopes, or if I just start thinking about what he said to me, it triggers it. Like I've been saying, that's the main thing bringing me in here—I just keep having these weird moments where my own office, or even my house, just feels completely unreal. Like I'm hovering and watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there in my own body. It's incredibly disturbing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like I'm watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. It happened just Tuesday when I was sitting at my desk. \n\nI was trying to review a client's Schedule C. And half the receipts are faded because he leaves them on the dashboard of his truck in the sun. Thermal paper doesn't survive that! I spend hours just trying to decipher if a receipt is from Home Depot or a gas station. And then he wants to deduct his dog's food as a security expense because the dog rides in the truck with him. I had to explain to him the IRS's definition of an \"ordinary and necessary\" business expense. They don't mess around with that stuff. Well, actually, I switched to Uline last year because Staples completely jacked up their free shipping minimums for business accounts. Uline is pretty good, though they send you this massive catalog every single month. It's literally the size of a phone book. \n\nDo they even make phone books anymore? Yeah, I'd say so. It's definitely the main trigger. Like I said, it's the whole reason I'm here... what brings me in is that I keep having these moments where my own house or office feels unreal. Like I'm watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. And it almost always happens after I've been thinking about him.

Twenty-two dollars! I mean, I know inflation is hitting everything, I see it in my clients' books all the time. The cost of goods sold is through the roof for everyone. But twenty-two dollars for deli meat? You might as well go out to a steakhouse. 

I ended up just getting some pre-packaged ham, which is full of sodium, I know, my doctor is always on me about my blood pressure. But anyway, I was making the sandwich, and I just happened to think about the guy—the client—and boom. Suddenly I'm not in my kitchen anymore. I'm floating up near the cabinets, watching myself put mayo on the bread. It's just... it's really messing with my head.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it really is. I've actually missed several client deadlines recently, which is... I mean, in my line of work, missing a deadline is a cardinal sin. But I just can't seem to focus. And speaking of deadlines, the whole e-file system has been so glitchy this year. Sometimes you hit submit on a return, and it just hangs there on this little spinning loading screen. Then you don't know if it actually went through to the IRS, so you have to go pull the acknowledgment report, which usually means printing it out for the physical file. 

And don't even get me started on printer ink. Have you bought toner lately? It's like buying liquid gold. I have a Brother laser printer—great machine, really reliable, almost never jams—but the name-brand toner cartridges are eighty bucks a pop now. I tried buying the cheap generic ones off Amazon a few months ago to save some money, but they left these horrible black streaks all down the left side of the page. You can't hand a client their 1040 with black streaks all over it, it looks completely unprofessional. I had to spend an hour taking the whole thing apart and cleaning the drum unit with a Q-tip and rubbing alcohol. You have to be really gentle when you do that, though, or you'll scratch the photosensitive roller, and then the whole unit is garbage... 

Wait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my ability to work. Yeah. So because of these episodes, I've actually completely stopped seeing people at the office. I just don't trust myself to handle their tax returns accurately when my brain is doing this, and honestly, I don't feel like I can keep the workplace safe right now. So I'm just... I'm really falling behind.

Like I'm hovering and watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there in my own body. It's incredibly disturbing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think seeing or thinking about the client who threatened you is what usually triggers these unreal feelings?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I'd say so. It's definitely the main trigger. Like I said, it's the whole reason I'm here... what brings me in is that I keep having these moments where my own house or office feels unreal. Like I'm watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. But they switched suppliers a few months ago, and now everything tastes like rubber. And the prices! I was looking at the roast beef the other day, and it was twenty-two dollars a pound. Twenty-two dollars! I mean, I know inflation is hitting everything, I see it in my clients' books all the time. The cost of goods sold is through the roof for everyone. But twenty-two dollars for deli meat? You might as well go out to a steakhouse. \n\nI ended up just getting some pre-packaged ham, which is full of sodium, I know, my doctor is always on me about my blood pressure. But anyway, I was making the sandwich, and I just happened to think about the guy—the client—and boom. Suddenly I'm not in my kitchen anymore. I'm floating up near the cabinets, watching myself put mayo on the bread. It's just... it's really messing with my head.\n\nDoctor: Are these unreal feelings affecting your ability to do your job as a tax preparer?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. Then you don't know if it actually went through to the IRS, so you have to go pull the acknowledgment report, which usually means printing it out for the physical file. \n\nAnd don't even get me started on printer ink. Have you bought toner lately? It's like buying liquid gold. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I don't know if it's affecting my relationships, really. I mean, I live alone, so it's not like I have a wife or kids around to notice me spacing out. My ex-wife took the dog when we split up, which was probably for the best because he was a Golden Retriever mix and he shed constantly. You'd vacuum the rug, and ten minutes later there'd be tumbleweeds of dog hair drifting across the hardwood. I bought one of those robot vacuums to try and keep up with it, you know, the ones that just bounce around the room? But it kept getting stuck under the edge of the sofa. It would just sit there beeping sadly until I came and rescued it. Plus, you have to empty the little dustbin on those things every single day, and if you forget, it just smears dirt around... 

But anyway, relationships. No, I haven't really noticed any issues there. I'm mostly just keeping to myself right now anyway, trying to figure out what's going on with my head.

He bought a boat last year and thinks he can write it off as a business expense because he entertained a client on it once. I had to explain to him—again—how the IRS views entertainment expenses now. It's just exhausting, you know? But yeah, anyway, these moments where I feel like I'm floating outside my own body... they're really starting to bother me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these moments where everything feels unreal?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been about two weeks now. Yeah, right around two weeks. It all started ever since that... that incident where a client threatened me right there in my office. Which, honestly, I still can't believe happened. I mean, I've been a tax preparer for nearly thirty years. You deal with upset people, sure. Nobody likes paying the IRS. But this guy... he was furious about an audit notice. And the thing is, I warned him! I told him last year, I said, \"You can't claim your golden retriever as a security system just because he barks at the mailman.\" But did he listen? No. They never listen. And then they get the letter from the IRS, and suddenly it's my fault. He came in, slamming his hands on my desk, knocking over my coffee—ruined a perfectly good stack of folders, by the way. My secretary, Brenda, she was terrified. Brenda's been with me for five years, lovely woman, makes the best snickerdoodles around the holidays. But she almost quit that day. I had to give her a bonus just to keep her. But yeah, ever since that afternoon with him... that's when this weird, floating, unreal feeling started happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms when you're having these unreal moments?\n\nPatient: Physical symptoms? Um... no, not that I can think of. I just get that weird floating feeling. Though, speaking of physical things, my lower back has been absolutely killing me lately, but that's probably just because of this new office chair I bought. I got it from this discount supply place over on 4th Street—Big Bob's Office Bonanza. Which is funny, because Big Bob is actually a very short man. Barely five foot four. Anyway, he promised me this chair had top-of-the-line lumbar support, but I swear it feels like a piece of plywood wrapped in cheap pleather. I really should have just ordered from the corporate catalog like Brenda told me to. She loves those catalogs. Every November, she goes through them with a pink highlighter, picking out sticky notes and those little rubber finger pads we use for sorting receipts. You know the ones? They look like tiny, bumpy thimbles. We must go through a hundred of those a season, what with all the paper cuts you get from W-2s. But, uh... what were you asking? Oh, right, my body during those moments. No, no racing heart or sweating or anything like that. Just the... the floating.\n\nDoctor: Do these unreal moments seem to happen at specific times or when you're doing certain things?\n\nPatient: Well, let's think... specific times. You know, I haven't really noticed a pattern. It just sort of happens whenever. Like yesterday, I was just looking out the window at the parking lot. Which, by the way, they finally repaved last weekend. It's about time, too. The potholes out there were getting ridiculous. I lost a hubcap in one back in November. A genuine Ford hubcap, not one of those cheap knockoffs you buy at the auto parts store. I tried to get the property manager, Gary, to reimburse me for it, but he just gave me the runaround. Gary's always doing that. He's one of those guys who talks a lot but never actually says anything, you know? He wears these incredibly loud ties—polka dots, neon stripes, you name it. I think he thinks it makes him look approachable, but really it just gives you a headache if you stare at him too long. Anyway, I was looking at the new asphalt, and suddenly I just felt like I was floating up near the ceiling tiles. So, no, I can't say it's tied to any specific activity. It just... sneaks up on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about the incident with the client who threatened you?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Yeah... yeah, I do. Actually, it's pretty much all the time now. Even when I'm just sitting at home in Roanoke, miles away from the office. I'll be in my living room, and I catch myself tracking every little sound. Like, I hear a car door shut down the street, and my head snaps up. Or I hear footsteps on the porch, and I'm instantly scanning the room, figuring out where the exits are, like I need to be ready for someone to come at me again. \n\nWhich is exhausting, let me tell you. Especially since our porch is made of this old wooden decking that creaks if a squirrel so much as looks at it. We've been meaning to get it replaced for years. My wife, Susan, she wants to go with that composite stuff, you know? The kind that's supposed to look like real wood but never rots or splinters. Trex, I think it's called. But I looked at the prices, and good lord, you'd think they were selling gold-plated two-by-fours. I told her we could just power-wash the old wood and put a nice coat of sealant on it, but she says it's an eyesore. She's been spending way too much time watching those home renovation shows on HGTV. You know the ones, where they buy a rundown shack and somehow turn it into a mansion in three weeks? It gives people completely unrealistic expectations about construction timelines. Our plumber took two months just to fix a leaky pipe in the guest bathroom! But anyway, yeah... I'm just constantly on edge, waiting for something bad to happen, even when I'm supposed to be relaxing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, people, or activities since the incident with the client who threatened you?\n\nPatient: Avoiding things? Um... no, not really. I mean, I still go to work, I still go to the grocery store. Though, speaking of the grocery store, I was at Kroger the other day, the one over on Elm Avenue. They completely rearranged the aisles. It's infuriating! I spent twenty minutes just trying to find the canned tomatoes. Why do they do that? You finally learn where everything is, and then some corporate manager decides the pasta needs to be next to the baking supplies instead of the sauces. It makes no sense. I was talking to the checkout girl about it—she's a sweet kid, maybe nineteen, always wears these oversized glasses. She said they do it so you have to walk past more items and hopefully buy things you didn't come in for. Which, I suppose, is a smart business strategy, but it's incredibly frustrating when you're just trying to make a quick chili. I make a really good chili, by the way. Secret is a dash of cinnamon. My mother taught me that. She used to make huge pots of it every winter... But, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, avoiding things. No, I haven't really been avoiding anything. I just go about my day, you know?\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have these unreal or floating feelings before the client threatened you in your office?\n\nPatient: Before? No, never. It's really just been going on for about two weeks now, ever since that client threatened me in my office. I've been a tax preparer for over two decades and I've never had someone blow up like that over a standard deduction. I mean, the tax code is the tax code, right? I don't write the IRS guidelines. \n\nSpeaking of the IRS, they changed the formatting on the 1040 again this year. It is a complete nightmare for our software. We use this program, TaxPro Elite, and every time there's a mid-season update, it crashes the entire office network. Last Tuesday, I spent three hours on hold with their tech support. Three hours! And the hold music is this awful, repetitive smooth jazz track. Just a synthesizer and a saxophone playing the same four notes on a loop. I swear they do it on purpose. It's a psychological tactic to make you hang up so they don't have to deal with you. I finally got a hold of a guy named Brent, who sounded like he was eating a sandwich the whole time he was talking to me. You could hear him chewing! It's just so unprofessional. \n\nBut anyway, what was the question? Oh, right, the floating thing. No, I never felt anything like that before. Just the last two weeks since the incident.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to avoid thinking or talking about what happened with that client who threatened you?\n\nPatient: Avoid thinking or talking about it? No, not really. I mean, I don't try to block it out or anything. If someone asks, I'll tell them. I was actually just on the phone with my brother, Dave, the other night, and we were talking for like an hour. Well, mostly he was talking. \n\nHe just bought a new truck. A Ford F-150. And let me tell you, the interest rates right now are absolutely criminal. As a tax guy, I look at the financing terms he agreed to, and it just makes my stomach turn. I told him, 'Dave, you're paying almost eight percent on a depreciating asset!' But he wouldn't listen. He just had to have the heated seats and the towing package. He doesn't even own a boat! What is he towing? The dealership saw him coming a mile away. They always do that trick where they focus on the monthly payment instead of the total cost of the vehicle. They extend the loan term out to 72 or 84 months so the monthly number looks smaller, but you end up paying thousands more in interest over the life of the loan. I tried to explain the amortization schedule to him, but he just glazed over. People never want to look at the math until tax season rolls around and they wonder where all their money went. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, avoiding talking about the client. No, no. I don't avoid it. I just don't really try to hide from it or anything.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience anything similar to these unreal or floating feelings during any other stressful or traumatic event in your life before this incident with the client?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't. Like, back in 2018, my water heater burst while I was at the office during the peak of tax season. Flooded my entire finished basement. Talk about stressful. The drywall was ruined, the carpet was soaking wet... we had to rip everything right down to the studs. \n\nAnd dealing with the insurance adjuster was an absolute nightmare. Guy's name was Greg, wore these awful yellow suspenders. He kept trying to claim it was a \"negligent maintenance\" issue and therefore wasn't fully covered under our specific policy tier.
